## 2026-08-21 — Yahoo: eight days of diagnosis ran on a status code because the body was thrown away

- **The conclusion was right and had never been verified.** `facts-of-record.md` #11 has said "refused at the app-entitlement level" since 2026-08-13. It is true — Yahoo returns `"This application is not authorized to perform this action."` But every prior assertion of it rested on `src/services/yahoo.js:54` throwing `Yahoo API error: ${res.status}` and discarding the response body. **At least four distinct failures return 403** — missing entitlement, blocked source IP, rate limiting, wrong-audience token — **with completely different remedies, and the status code cannot tell them apart.** Being right by inference for eight days is not the same as knowing, and it cost a week of "wait for Yahoo" that could have been an hour of certainty.
- **The founder's question was the useful one.** *Two approval letters and it still doesn't work* is not a complaint to absorb; it is evidence that the standing explanation fits badly. An entitlement story predicts that an approval changes something. Two approvals changing nothing should have triggered a re-examination earlier than it did.
- **The competing hypothesis was worth taking seriously and was wrong.** A blocked datacenter IP explains "approvals don't help" perfectly, and Omen runs on Hostinger. It was killed in ten seconds: an unauthenticated call from `omen-prod` egress `2.25.182.1` returns an ordinary **401**, identical to a residential IP. **Cheap disproof beats a plausible theory**, and the value of holding it was never that it was right — it was that testing it closed the last alternative, which is what makes the Aug 28 escalation a plan rather than a guess.
- **The token was never the problem, and now that is settled too.** The stored token had expired on 2026-08-14; the proactive refresh fired mid-call and Yahoo refused the **fresh** token. Valid credentials, fresh token, clean IP, correct app, executed contract, still refused. `P1-YahooReauth`'s token branch is closed for good.
- **Five apps, not two — and the console answered the founder's real fear.** The developer account holds five apps all named "Omen"; three were unknown to this repo. **The Fantasy Sports permission is not rendered at all on the other four.** Yahoo's own UI names `ZcZJXm8V` as the fantasy app, which is the deployed one. **Do not delete the unused apps** — deleting one is what destroyed the previous grant, and tidiness here has already cost this project an approval cycle once.
- **Attribution was gated on the Yahoo flags rather than hardcoded, on honesty grounds.** The contract requires attribution "wherever Yahoo Fantasy Information is displayed". Omen displays none. An unconditional footer line would put a false claim on every page, beside a non-affiliation disclaimer that is the *opposite* clause. Deriving it from `YAHOO_CONNECTIONS_ENABLED` (web) and `ConnectProvider.yahoo.availability` (native) means it appears on the same flip that makes Yahoo real. **Over-inclusive once live, by choice: over-attributing is not a breach, under-attributing is.**
- **Native attribution used the existing Help + Support surface instead of building a Legal screen.** A new screen would need its own screen contract and Figma approval under `omen-native-delivery-governance-v1.md`. The clause says "About, Legal, **or similar informational section**" — Help + Support already is one. **The governance gate was satisfied by not needing it**, which is cheaper than seeking an exception.
- **Two "looks wrong, therefore is wrong" errors in one session; the second is recorded because it nearly shipped.** `league_id: "yahoo"` was called a data bug on sight. It is a documented NOT NULL sentinel that `hasUsableLeagueId()` explicitly rejects. **Same failure shape as reading a 403 without its body: judging a value without reading the code that writes it.** Retracted before it reached the ledger, and the retraction is kept rather than deleted.
- **Pre-existing failures were proved, not argued — and the F9 lesson held.** Two iOS contrast tests fail on the help surfaces this session edited. Rather than reasoning "the attribution renders nothing while Yahoo is on hold, therefore not mine", the diff was stashed and the class re-run against clean `main`: identical failures. The convenient belief was correct, which is exactly why it needed checking.

## 2026-08-20 — F9: a deferred run is not a passed run, and the difference was a compile failure

- **The rule F9 enforces.** `Live` requires an explicit backend truth field. Missing, unknown, or self-contradictory modes fail closed to `Unverified` or an error — they never inherit a live badge from a platform name or a 200 response. Both native clients previously minted one synthetic `Live league data` signal for **every** renderable success, which is the exact failure mode F9 exists to prevent: an honest-looking live label attached to data nobody verified.
- **`Mock` and `Demo` are now different words.** Native `.mock` used to render a `Demo` badge. They are different claims — fixture data versus sample data — and collapsing them means one of the two labels is always lying.
- **The deferred verification earned its cost immediately.** `4c4a0fe` shipped an iOS test target that **did not compile**: four Swift triple-quote literals written inline. No iOS assertion in that commit had ever run. **`DEFERRED-CI` records that a run has not happened; it does not record that the code would survive one.** Those read alike in a ledger and are not the same claim. Nothing in the Windows session was dishonest — the deferral was recorded correctly — but the distance between "not yet run" and "would have passed" turned out to be a hard build error, in the tests that carry the whole point of the task.
- **Pre-existing failures were proved, not argued.** Two iOS contrast tests fail. The cheap move is to reason from the diff — F9 touched no contextual-help code, therefore not F9. Instead the class was run at pre-F9 base `33fcfe6` in a clean worktree and failed identically. **"Unrelated to my change" is a hypothesis until a base run makes it a fact**, and it is the single most convenient thing to be wrong about when closing a task.
- **Client strictness deliberately left uneven.** Web trims and lowercases `mode`; native compares exactly. A hypothetical `"Live"` would pass on web and fail closed on native. Backend emitters are canonical lowercase, so this is theoretical — and where it does diverge, native errs toward refusing to claim live. Left as-is and recorded rather than harmonised, because the asymmetry runs in the safe direction.
- **What is still unproven, stated because a green suite is not a rendering.** No human VoiceOver or TalkBack pass over the labeled states, and no screenshot of the new Mock/Demo/Unverified treatments. The Compose tests assert the text exists in the tree, not that it can be read. `O7` already produced the counterexample — a background-token defect that survived 276 passing tests across both platforms.

## 2026-08-19 — O2: the deploy pipeline destroys the artifact you would roll back to

- **What the documentation half found, which is the reason `O2` says "test it before you need it".** `deploy.yml` publishes exactly one tag per image — `ghcr.io/justinduverge-design/omen:main` — with no immutable per-build tag. `:main` is overwritten on every deploy, so **"redeploy the previous tag" names nothing.** The deploy step then runs `docker image prune -f` immediately after `pull` + `up -d`, which removes the image just replaced. **Both halves of the obvious rollback — redeploy the old tag, or retag the cached image — are unavailable, and the second is unavailable because the pipeline deliberately deletes it.**
- **What still works, and why it is not good enough.** GHCR retains untagged manifests, so rollback by digest is real. But nothing in the pipeline records a digest, and reading them needs a `read:packages` token — a plain `gh` token returns 403, confirmed live 2026-08-19. So the recovery path depends on a value nobody wrote down, discovered during an outage, through a UI. **A rollback procedure whose first step is a scavenger hunt is not a rollback procedure.**
- **Recommended, not applied.** One line per image (`:sha-${{ github.sha }}`) makes rollback a tag change readable off the commit history; dropping or time-scoping `docker image prune -f` restores the local fallback. **Deployment workflow changes are founder-gated**, so this ships as a recommendation with the exact diff rather than a change.
- **The mobile answer is now written down rather than assumed.** There is no mobile rollback. Halting distribution stops new installs; it does not remove or downgrade an installed app. `O7`'s forced-update gate is the mitigation, and its ordering constraint is part of the record: **store URLs first, minimum version second.** Reversed, a bad build becomes a total lockout — a correct prompt attached to a dead end, with no route through the app.

## 2026-08-20 — Omen adopts the L0-owned Valor Brain metadata v1 contract

- **Adoption is opt-in, not a mass conversion.** Only pages declaring `metadata_profile: valor-brain/v1` are governed; existing product Markdown and the canonical sprint/status model remain unchanged.
- **Omen stays independently valid.** The L0 schema and zero-dependency validator are mirrored byte-for-byte under `Blueprints/specs/` and `scripts/`, while `RESOLVER.md`, kickoff, and Done guidance make the local route executable in a standalone clone.
- **The O2 pilot is the first governed page, not a new authority source.** It remains `REVIEW_ONLY`; canonical sprint, runbook, workflow, and evidence sources win on conflict. Its task remains `IN_PROGRESS`, its code change is `APPLIED`, and its founder-only live exercise remains `NOT_RUN`.
- **A stale gate found by trying to pass it.** `Blueprints/done/release-done.md` still carried a ⛔ HARD-BLOCKED banner citing a GitHub Actions billing hold that **was retracted on 2026-08-01 and never existed**. It would have blocked a legitimate Release Done closure on a known-false premise, and had done so for 18 days. **Nobody reads a gate they are not currently trying to pass**, which is why this surfaced during `O2` and not during the reconciliation two hours earlier — that pass compared records against GitHub, and this was a record contradicting a *retraction* inside the repo, which no checker covers.
- **What `O2` still owes, and why neither half is delegable.** The rollback owner must be a named person — at 2am "ops" is not an answer to "who do I call" — and the path must be executed once against a non-critical deploy. Rolling back production is an action-level founder approval per the safety gates. **Until the exercise runs, the runbook is a plausible procedure, not a tested one**, and the failure modes that matter are precisely the ones a dry read does not surface.


## 2026-08-19 — Known-issues reconciliation: the contradiction was in the higher-authority file, and nothing was watching

- **What the pass was.** Founder-requested after `O7` merged: check every `known_issues.md` entry against `main`, merged PRs, and open issues rather than re-reading the file. Sort into already-fixed, still-open-and-known, and still-open-but-buried.
- **The finding that matters.** `facts-of-record.md` #11 asserted Yahoo API access *"was re-approved… the developer application is live again"* and pointed at a stale OAuth token as the live problem. `known_issues.md` had recorded the opposite since **2026-08-13**, with live evidence — 403 on all four probe calls including public `/game/nfl`, entitlement refused for app `ZcZJXm8V`, issue #308 open — and explicitly marked the token diagnosis `SUPERSEDED`. The two files disagreed for six days, and **the wrong version was in the file with higher authority**, which agents read earlier in the kickoff order. Corrected, provenance retained.
- **The generalisable failure: a correction was written where it was discovered, not everywhere it was asserted.** The 2026-08-13 session found the entitlement problem and recorded it accurately — in the file it happened to be working in. It did not sweep for other places asserting the old fact. **When a fact of record is superseded, amending the fact-of-record entry is part of the fix, not follow-up work.** This is the same shape as the M1-Screen lesson (repo contract amended, Figma copy left stale) and the nflverse lesson (cron fixed, `matchupService.js` left broken) — a fix applied at the discovery site while an identical assertion sat untouched elsewhere. Third instance in a month.
- **Nothing was watching, and that is the real gap.** `scripts/check-sprint-staleness.js` compares sprint keys to merged PR titles. Nothing compares `known_issues.md` to `facts-of-record.md`; nothing compares either to GitHub issues. The contradiction was not missed through carelessness — no mechanism existed that could have seen it.
- **`S8` was finished on 2026-08-11 and was still being offered as an available P1 pull on 2026-08-19**, including in that morning's re-derived inbox queue. Zero Dependabot PRs were open; all six were resolved exactly as S8's own verdicts prescribed. **The staleness script is structurally blind to it**: it matches sprint keys against PR titles, and Dependabot titles are `build(deps): …` and never carry a key. Closed as `COMPLETED`, with the reason it survived recorded on the item.
- **What changed, so this is visible without reading the repo.** Four issues that existed only in `known_issues.md` are now GitHub issues #338 (fonts never acquired), #339 (backend Sentry breadcrumbs unscrubbed), #340 (contrast 4.43:1), #341 (Android light-mode status bar). **Standing rule adopted:** a real unresolved known issue gets a GitHub issue and carries its number in the heading; if it is not worth an issue, it is not worth an entry. The founder's stated reason is the right one — GitHub is what they can actually see and steer from.
- **On GDPR, since it came up.** `src/omen_gdpr.js` was listed as present; it was deleted by PR #119 as an **orphan that was never mounted**, so no capability was lost. The live surface is `src/routes/userPrivacy.js` (`export`, `delete`, `consent`, `legal-acceptance`, all behind `requireAuth`, two test files). **Recorded as technical mechanisms only — not a compliance determination**, which depends on policy wording and retention terms and is not an engineering call to make.


## 2026-08-19 — `O7` ships the forced-update mechanism deliberately inert, and the documented iOS test command is wrong

- **The gate is built and blocks nobody.** `O7` adds `GET /api/system/min-version` plus a native gate on both platforms, and sets both minimums to `0.1.0` — exactly the version both apps currently ship — so no user is blocked by this change. That is the intended end state for now, not an unfinished one. The item's own **Do not touch** line forbids forcing an update without a working store listing to update to, and neither listing is live (`R3`/`R4` open). Shipping the mechanism early and arming it later is the right order: the lever has to already be in the build on users' phones before it can ever help, because a phone running today's build can only be reached by a check today's build already performs. A gate added *after* a bad ship is worthless.
- **Fail-open is implemented three times over, on purpose.** The service reports `ok` for an unknown platform or unparseable version; both clients collapse network/non-2xx/undecodable to `Unavailable`; both view models handle `Unavailable` in the same branch as `Ok`; and the initial state is `passed`, so the app is never blocked while the check is in flight. The asymmetry is deliberate — a gate that wrongly blocks is worse than one that wrongly passes, because the wrongly-blocked user has no recourse inside the app and the failure is invisible to us.
- **Arming it is a founder action with an ordering constraint.** Fill `OMEN_IOS_APP_STORE_URL`, confirm the Play URL resolves, *then* raise `MIN_APP_VERSION_IOS` / `MIN_APP_VERSION_ANDROID`. Raising the minimum first produces the correct prompt attached to a button that goes nowhere — a self-inflicted lockout with no in-app escape.
- **Found: the DoD's documented iOS local substitute cannot pass a merged test.** `Blueprints/definition-of-done.md` prescribes `xcodebuild test … CODE_SIGNING_ALLOWED=NO CODE_SIGNING_REQUIRED=NO`. Run verbatim, four `KeychainSessionStoreTests` fail with `errSecMissingEntitlement` (-34018) — the Keychain needs a keychain-access-group entitlement that only exists on a signed bundle. The same command without the signing flags passes all five. `S5` added those tests on 2026-08-18 and recorded them green, so this is the documented command being stale relative to the suite, not a regression and not a defect in `KeychainSessionStore`. **This is the second time in two days that the S5 work surfaced this exact toolchain trap** (see the 2026-08-18 skill-ledger row, which recorded it but did not amend the doc). Recording it twice without fixing the doc is how it reaches a third session: the correction belongs in `definition-of-done.md` itself, and until it lands, any agent following the doc literally will read four red Keychain tests as a real failure.

## 2026-08-18 — Two branches of `O1b` truth reconciled: the ratified Sentry SaaS half was never merged

- **What happened.** On 2026-08-17 the founder ratified Option D (the split destination — Sentry SaaS free tier for mobile/browser, GlitchTip on Command Center for backend), recorded in `Direction/reviews/2026-08-17-o1b-error-tracking-comparison.md` and on the branch that became PRs [#327](https://github.com/justinduverge-design/omen/pull/327) and [#328](https://github.com/justinduverge-design/omen/pull/328). That branch pinned in `agent_inbox.md`: **"Two PRs must merge first, in order — #327 then #328"** — before any session started the GlitchTip build. A separate session built and closed `O1b` (PRs #329, #330) without merging either first. Its closure narrative — GlitchTip on Command Center, proven end to end — is accurate as far as it goes, but it is silent on the Sentry SaaS half, because the record of that half only ever existed on the two unmerged branches. **`main`'s `O1b` closure was correct and incomplete at the same time**, the same failure shape this log has already named twice this week (the Slops OS infrastructure fleet, 2026-08-17; `O5`'s backup verification, same entry) — real, verified work sitting outside the trail a status claim gets read from.
- **Reconciling now, not reopening.** Per `Direction/status-model.md`, `CLOSED` is terminal — this entry supplements `O1b`'s closed record rather than editing its `Status`/`Blocked by` fields. `current_sprint.md` gets a short addendum instead.
- **The Sentry SaaS half, for the record.** A new org **`valor-ventures-llc`** was created 2026-08-17 (the June-instrumented `SENTRY_DSN` had always shipped blank — no prior account existed to lose). **⚠️ Both halves of that parenthetical are DISPROVEN — corrected 2026-08-21 from the running production containers.** `SENTRY_DSN` on KVM1 was **not blank**: it held the literal placeholder `paste_the_value_here` with a real Sentry DSN glued onto the end, 115 characters, byte-identical in `omen_api` and `omen_cron`. That DSN references org **`o4511559534641152`** — **not** `valor-ventures-llc` (`o4511928445960192`), so **a prior backend Sentry account did exist**, predating the 2026-08-17 org. **The "always shipped blank" belief was accidentally true in effect and false in fact**, which is exactly why it survived: the malformed value is not a legal URL, so `@sentry/node` built no transport and behaved indistinguishably from a blank DSN — while `enabled: Boolean(process.env.SENTRY_DSN)` reported `true`. Nobody was wrong to observe "nothing is arriving"; the error was inferring the cause from the symptom without reading the value. Found during `O8`; tracked and closed as issue [#354](https://github.com/justinduverge-design/omen/issues/354). Free Developer plan, 14-day unlimited trial reverting to 5,000 errors/month, 30-day retention, 1 seat, no card on file, ~2026-08-31. Three projects: **React (web)**, **iOS**, **Android** — platform choices deliberate (Android SDK, not Kotlin, for ANR/ProGuard mapping; React, not the React-Router-framework preset, since the frontend is a Vite SPA using `react-router` as a library). **Web verified end-to-end with zero code changes**: `VITE_SENTRY_DSN` set in root `.env.local` (mind `frontend/vite.config.js:10`'s `envDir` pointing at repo root, not `frontend/`), a deliberate uncaught error captured and transmitted, event `ab02073085b44260a949a8028fc22894`. iOS/Android DSNs exist but are unverified and unused — no SDK in either native app yet; that's `O6`'s job, noted on its own record below. **No Express/backend Sentry project was created** — Option D keeps bursty backend volume off the metered SaaS path entirely, which is the point of the split.
- **What that verification found, and PR #328 fixes.** Running a live payload through the frontend's `beforeSend` — not reading the scrubber, executing it — surfaced that `frontend/src/lib/sentry.js` scrubbed query parameters with `SENSITIVE_KEY_PATTERN`, which has no `code` and no `state`, while the backend's `SENSITIVE_QUERY_PARAMETER_PATTERN` (`src/middleware/sentry.js:8`) does. Reachable: Yahoo and Discord OAuth returns land on frontend routes carrying `?code=&state=`. **This log's own OAuth-artifacts entry claims "Sentry additionally scrubs OAuth `code` and `state` query parameters" without distinguishing the two halves — the claim was true only of the backend.** PR #328 adds a matching `SENSITIVE_QUERY_PARAMETER_PATTERN` to the frontend, plus `scrubBreadcrumbUrls` for the `url`/`from`/`to` breadcrumb fields (a second gap in the same reachable path, found while fixing the first — breadcrumb data is scrubbed by key name, and a URL living under a non-sensitive key was never examined). Verified against the live client's own `beforeSend`/`beforeBreadcrumb`: `code`, `state`, `espn_s2` all `[scrubbed]` in both the event URL and all three breadcrumb fields, benign params preserved, zero leaks across the probe set; ESPN-credential URLs still drop the event entirely; backend `npm test` 563/563, frontend build clean, `npm audit` 0.
- **Still open: the backend has the identical breadcrumb-URL gap.** `src/middleware/sentry.js`'s breadcrumb scrubbing is the same key-name-only match, so a Node-side breadcrumb carrying an OAuth return URL under `url`/`from`/`to` would survive uncorrected. Lower severity than the frontend case — Node breadcrumbs are less likely to carry a browser OAuth-callback URL — but not zero. Filed in `known_issues.md`, deliberately unfixed here: security code earns its own test, and the backend has a real test runner to write one in.
- **Downstream.** `O6` (native crash reporting) already has two provisioned, unverified Sentry projects (iOS, Android) waiting for it — noted on `O6`'s own record so a future session doesn't recreate them.

## 2026-08-18 — "Closed" proved the tool, not the payoff: queued the two follow-ons `O1b` actually needed

- **Founder pushback, and it was correct.** `O1b`'s done-when was proven by hand — a synthetic error sent by curl from a terminal, not a real failure from Omen's own code. That's a legitimate proof of the exact `Done when:` clause `O1b` stated (the tool catches an error within 60s with a usable stack trace), but it is a materially smaller claim than "GlitchTip catches Omen's real errors," and closing the item without a visible next step let those two claims blur together.
- **Added `O8`** — wire real backend error paths (starting with the adapter failures `O1b`'s own canonical example named) into GlitchTip. Specified a direct-HTTP integration over adding a Sentry SDK dependency, since this session already proved the wire protocol by hand and `package.json`/dependencies sit on this repo's standing do-not-touch list — the hand-rolled path avoids a founder-approval gate that an SDK dependency would require.
- **Added `O9`** — route GlitchTip issues through the Layer 5 Discord dispatcher that already exists in the Slops OS infrastructure fleet. Founder's framing: the two are "cousins" that should help each other, since the dispatcher already has the proven notification path and GlitchTip is simply a signal source it doesn't know about yet.
- **Corrected `S4`**, which turned out to be carrying the same class of staleness this repo keeps re-encountering: its scope referenced "Sentry payloads once O1 lands," but `O1` (Kuma/Beszel) never touched Sentry-shaped data — that was always `O1b`'s job — and `O1b` closing didn't create a payload-emitting pipeline either. Retargeted at `O8`; the log/error-envelope half of `S4`'s scope was never actually blocked and is called out as testable now.
- **Followed through the same session with the operational-resilience tests the pushback asked for**, rather than leaving it as a queued intention. `O1b`'s closure had proven GlitchTip *accepts and stores* an error; it had not proven GlitchTip *survives* anything — every other Slops OS layer (Kuma, Beszel, Steward's jobs, Sentinel's sensors) only counted as done after a proven kill-and-recover cycle plus a proven reboot survival, and GlitchTip had gotten neither. Both are now proven: a deliberate container stop/restart (site genuinely went down, recovered in ~18s, data intact) and a second, clean Command Center reboot — this time confirming the morning's cgroup `mem_limit` fix is durable rather than a one-time patch, since all three containers came back with the correct memory caps already baked in, with no manual `--force-recreate` needed. Recorded on `O1b`'s entry in `current_sprint.md`. **One gap intentionally left open and named rather than quietly closed:** GlitchTip has no Uptime Kuma monitor watching it, unlike the three Omen endpoints — nothing currently notices if it goes down on its own. Needs Kuma admin credentials or a two-minute manual step; not something to fabricate a workaround for.

## 2026-08-17 — The Slops OS infrastructure fleet was real and undocumented in this repo; migrating it surfaced a live status defect

- **A parallel body of infrastructure work existed entirely outside this git repo.** A "temporary field document" in Google Drive tracked a six-layer Raspberry Pi fleet build (management plane, observability, Steward automation, Sentinel security observation, Discord alerting, a Pi-hole DNS lab) that this repo only ever knew the smallest slice of — `O1` covers Layer 2 alone. Migrated the durable architecture, proven decisions, and guardrails into `Blueprints/specs/infrastructure/slops-os-raspberry-pi-fleet-v1.md`, condensed from ~4,500 lines of dated checkpoint evidence. The Drive document remains the granular historical record; this repo file is now the source that should be kept current.
- **Deliberately excluded from the migration:** two companion "interview" documents in the same Drive folder contained home-network and personal-context detail — WAN IP address, room-by-room ethernet layout, family device-access preferences, Valor Ventures business-document categories (LLC formation, EIN, banking records). None of it is operationally load-bearing for anyone reading this repo, and this repo's git history is not the right place for it. Only architecture, proven technical decisions, and guardrails were brought in.
- **The migration surfaced a real, live status defect: `O5` was wrong.** `Direction/current_sprint.md` listed `O5` (Supabase backup and restore verification) as `Status: READY`, gated on `FOUNDER_APPROVAL`, with the note "never verified — an untested backup is not a backup." The Drive tracker proves this was fully built and proven on **2026-08-11** — six days before this discovery — including a full disaster-recovery drill with exact row-count verification against an isolated restore target, and a Supabase Auth export that goes beyond what the default `supabase db dump` covers. `O5` is now closed with that evidence. See `Direction/sprints_completed.md`.
- **Why this matters beyond one stale line.** `agent_inbox.md` already tracks seven prior instances of this repo's queue advertising shipped work as pullable, all within this repo's own git history. This is a different failure shape — not a handoff written before a merge landed, but an entire body of real, verified work that happened in a system this repo's status-tracking process never touches at all. The existing staleness tooling (`scripts/check-sprint-staleness.js`, matched against PR titles) structurally cannot catch this class of gap, the same limitation already noted for Figma-evidenced design items (`M10-DesignLaneStaleness`). Worth naming as its own risk: **any status claim sourced only from this repo's own git/PR history is blind to work proven in an adjacent system** (Drive docs, the Pi fleet itself, or elsewhere). No fix is proposed here beyond the fact of the gap; a mechanical check across sources this different is a harder problem than the PR-title matcher.
- **One coincidental but genuinely useful connection found in the process:** Layer 2's live KVM2 discovery independently confirmed the public Nginx serving `openclaw.slopssaloon.com` → `127.0.0.1:3200` that this repo's own `S6` item already tracks as an open retirement question — corroborating evidence from a completely independent audit, not a new finding.

## 2026-08-17 — O1b closed: GlitchTip on Command Center, not KVM2, and two real defects fixed along the way

- **Checking the sprint doc's own resource estimate changed the hosting decision.** `O1b` estimated GlitchTip at "roughly 1–2 GB" and named KVM2 as the fallback if Command Center couldn't take it. The primary source (`glitchtip.com/documentation/install`) states 256–512 MB is enough and arm64 is officially supported — materially lighter than assumed. That put it well inside Command Center's 3.2 GB available headroom, so it went there: already reachable, no new access to provision, and it keeps the observability hub and the error tracker on hardware that's already proven stable for 9+ days. KVM2 SSH access was fixed anyway (see below) since it surfaced as a separate, useful gap.

- **A stray SSH habit, not a missing key, was blocking KVM2.** `~/.ssh/config` on the working Mac already documented `Host kvm2 omen-ai` → `User ubuntu`, `HostName 100.67.187.57`. Earlier attempts had used the raw Tailscale hostname (`srv1647690`) with a guessed username (`justin`, correct for KVM1 but not KVM2), which bypassed the config's `User` directive entirely. No credential was missing or needed adding — using the documented alias worked immediately.

- **GlitchTip's `smtp://` URL scheme does not imply implicit TLS from the port number.** Port 465 was configured (Resend's own recommended default), but GlitchTip opened a plaintext SMTP connection into a port that only speaks TLS from the first byte, and the connection hung until socket timeout. This is exactly what surfaced mid-session as "registration taking absolutely forever" — the account was actually created successfully; only the verification-email send hung. Confirmed root cause with a direct `smtplib` reproduction inside the container: plaintext-on-465 times out (`SMTPServerDisconnected: ... timed out`), while both implicit-SSL-on-465 and STARTTLS-on-587 complete a real handshake against Resend. Fixed by switching to port 587. **General lesson: a bare `smtp://host:port` pairing is not self-describing about TLS mode — verify the actual protocol handshake, not just that the TCP port is reachable, before calling an email integration done.**

- **`mem_limit` in the compose file is a request, not a guarantee — Docker silently drops it if the kernel can't enforce it.** Command Center's stock Raspberry Pi OS kernel had cgroup memory accounting disabled, so the 768m/256m/768m caps set on GlitchTip's containers were silently discarded (`docker compose up` did print a warning — `Your kernel does not support memory limit capabilities` — but only at explicit `up` time, not during the daemon's own restart-on-boot path, which is why it was initially missed). `docker stats`' `MEM USAGE / LIMIT` display is not reliable evidence either way here: with no real limit set, it falls back to showing total host memory as the ceiling, which reads as plausible even when nothing is actually capped. The authoritative check is `docker inspect --format '{{.HostConfig.Memory}}'` (0 means no limit) cross-checked against the live cgroup file (`/sys/fs/cgroup/.../memory.max`, literally the string `max` when unset). Fixed with the standard Raspberry Pi OS fix — `cgroup_enable=memory cgroup_memory=1` appended to the single line in `/boot/firmware/cmdline.txt` (backed up first; this file must never gain a stray newline) — followed by a reboot.
  - **A second, less obvious gotcha: the reboot alone did not apply the limits.** Docker's daemon restarts existing containers on boot using their already-baked `HostConfig`, which was fixed at whatever time they were last *created* — in this case, before the kernel fix existed. A plain restart does not re-resolve the compose file's resource constraints against newly-available kernel capabilities. `docker compose up -d --force-recreate` was required to actually bake the caps in. Re-verified authoritatively afterward: `HostConfig.Memory` reads exactly 805306368 / 268435456 bytes, matching 768m / 256m.
  - **The reboot itself was low-risk in practice, not just in theory.** All seven containers on the host — Pi-hole, Beszel, Beszel-agent, Uptime Kuma, and GlitchTip's three — came back cleanly on their own via each compose file's `restart: unless-stopped`, confirmed by SSH reachability within ~20 seconds and full service health shortly after.

- **`O1b`'s done-when bar was proven with direct database evidence, not inference from a 200 response.** A synthetic `ESPNMalformedResponseError` — deliberately mirroring `O1b`'s own canonical example ("a user just got a 500 on `POST /api/omen/mvp-move` because ESPN returned malformed JSON") — was POSTed to the ingest API from an external host (not the Pi itself, to prove the real network path). The ingest endpoint's 200 response only proves acceptance, not processing, so the actual check queried `issue_events_issueevent` in Postgres directly: the event was stored, grouped into an issue, and carried its full two-frame stack trace, all within 1 second of sending. Resource cost recorded as ~300 MB RAM and ~2.9 GB disk, both well inside Command Center's headroom.

- **`O6` (native crash reporting) is now half-unblocked.** It listed `TASK-O1b` as a blocker for both platforms; that's resolved now that a working error-tracking backend exists. Its `Status` stays `BLOCKED` overall because `Done when:` requires both iOS and Android, and the iOS half still needs `TASK-R3-BUILD-iOS` to reach a signed build before dSYM symbolication is possible. The Android half is now agent-buildable on its own.

## 2026-08-16 — Four founder calls on the Trade and League contracts

- **The Trade verdict's fourth label lands on the server, not in the client.** `POST /api/trade/compare` gains `contract_version: "trade-compare.v1"` and an explicit evaluability signal derived from the `missing_projection_count` `compareTrade()` already computes; native then maps four-to-four onto the approved §9.2 vocabulary. **Rejected: inferring "Insufficient data" from `confidence: "low"` on the client.** One bench player without a projection is not an un-evaluable trade, and that threshold would be a second copy of an evaluability judgement that belongs to the engine — the drift slice D's "render the server's own answer" rule exists to prevent. The change is additive and the existing three values stay, because `TradeAnalyzer.jsx` and `lib/tradeShare.js` read `result.verdict` directly.

- **"Personalize" waits for real league context rather than shipping scoped to scoring format.** `/compare` accepts `send`, `receive`, and `scoring_format` only. A Personalize affordance that changes scoring format alone is honest in its fine print and misleading in its label — users read "personalized" as league-aware. The personalized half of `M5` slice G is **blocked** until the route accepts league/roster context (visual briefs §8.3: league scoring, roster construction, dynasty/keeper). The neutral path is unaffected and stays public, free, and account-free — which is Trade's actual job as the front door.

- **The empty "Around the League" section is the v1 build target.** The League Primary frames drew a populated activity feed for which no backend source exists. Both platforms are redrawn and renamed *"standings live, activity empty (v1)"*, and the populated composition is preserved as an explicitly-labelled future state (iOS `90:2`, Android `90:8`) rather than deleted. **The reasoning is the Draft precedent:** an implementer builds what the frame shows, which is exactly how a cut feature survived in this file for five days. A frame that depicts a capability we do not have is a defect, not an aspiration.

- **Off-season standings use clean omission for 1.0.** `league-standings.v1` returns `standings: []` and carries no prior-season history, so the "supported prior-season final standings" half of the alternate frame is not buildable; it is parked as a future backend capability. Per facts-of-record #10 this off-season state is what a user actually sees today, so it is the state to make good rather than an edge case.

- **Three items minted from the audit:** `M1-QA-EvidenceGate` (the §4 gate covers all eight flows; only two have QA records), `M9-NativeScreenBacklog` (Waiver Analysis, Start/Sit detail, Ledger detail, and the switcher sheet are approved designs with no delivery item anywhere), and `M10-DesignLaneStaleness` (extend the staleness check to items whose evidence is a Figma node, the gap that let the eighth incident through).

- **Still outstanding: ratification of the two screen contracts themselves.** The decisions above are applied and recorded on the QA & Evidence records; every frame still carries its `PROPOSAL — AWAITING FOUNDER APPROVAL` badge, and slices F and G remain blocked.

All three are in `Blueprints/handoffs/frontend-to-backend.md` and all three await a founder call before `M5` slices F and G.
